i,j,k
, then
X_{i,k}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Consider the two following trees
T_{1},T_{2}
with a common subtree on
\{b,c\}
:
i
j
a
b
c
j
k
b
c
d

The only amalgamation of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
over
\{b,c\}
is the following tree
T
i
j
k
a
b
c
d

which must belong to
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
by condition
d
. The induced subtree on
\{a,c,d\}
in
T
is isomorphic to
X_{i,k}
, which by condition
b
implies that
X_{i,k}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.
∎

We say that a color
k\in[n]

appears
in a subclass
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
if there exists a tree with node(s) colored
k
in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. Alternatively,
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
has color
k
if
k
appears.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
i
and
j
both appear in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, then at least one of
X_{i,j}
and
X_{j,i}
is in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By
b
, the two following trees belong to
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
:
i
a
b
j
b
c

The only two amalgamations over the one-point tree on
b
are
i
j
b
c
a
j
i
a
c
b

at least one of which is in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
by
d
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

If
X_{i,j},X_{j,i}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
for distinct
i,j
, then
X_{i,i},X_{j,j}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Consider the two trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
i
j
i
b
c
d
a
j
i
j
b
c
d
a

By Proposition
4.1
,
T_{1}
is the unique amalgamation of
T_{1}\setminus a\cong X_{j,i}
and
T_{1}\setminus c\cong X_{i,j}
over
T_{1}\setminus\{a,c\}
, so we must have
T_{1}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
by condition
d
. Similarly,
T_{2}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. Finally, notice that the induced subtrees on
a,c,d
in
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are
X_{i,i}
and
X_{j,j}
respectively, implying that
X_{i,i},X_{j,j}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
by
b
.
∎

Let
X_{n}=\{X_{i,j}:i,j\in[n]\}
be the set of trees with three leaves in
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
, and
A
be a subset of
X_{n}
. Let
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
denote the corresponding subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
of all structures whose (only) three-element substructures are those in
A
. Clearly, if
A
is nonempty, then
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
is unique. If
A
is empty, then
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
is empty or contains structures with at most one or two leaves; we denote the latter classes as
\mathfrak{T}_{\leq 1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{\leq 2}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

The subclass
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
is Fraïssé if and only if it satisfies the conditions in Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
,
5.3
.

Proof.

The converse is clear, so we show the forward direction. Conditions
a
and
b
are satisfied by construction, while
c
is inherited from
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
. It thus suffices to check
d
. Let
T_{1},T_{2}
be tree structures in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
with common substructure
T
. We proceed by induction on
|T_{2}\setminus T|
.

If
|T_{2}\setminus T|=0
, then
T_{1}
is the desired amalgamation. Otherwise, by the inductive hypothesis, there exists an amalgamation
T^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}\setminus a
for any
a\in T_{2}\setminus T
. By the amalgamation property of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
, there is an amalgamation
T^{\prime\prime}\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
such that the restriction of
T^{\prime\prime}
to
T_{1}\cup T_{2}\setminus a
is isomorphic to
T^{\prime}
. Now, let
N(a)
denote the node adjacent to
a
and
C(a)
denote its color. For
b\in T_{1}
, if there is
c\in T_{2}
such that
X_{C(a),C(b)},X_{C(b),C(c)}\in A
(resp.
X_{C(c),C(b)},X_{C(b),C(a)}\in A
), then by Lemma
5.1
, we have
X_{C(a),C(c)}\in A
(resp.
X_{C(c),C(a)}\in A
), implying that no new three-element structures are introduced in
A
by
T^{\prime\prime}
. Otherwise, let
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\}
be the set of
b_{i}\in T_{1}
such that such
c
does not exist and either the simple path from the root to
b_{i}
contains
a
or the path from the root to
a
contains
b_{i}
, since only such
b_{i}
may potentially introduce new three-element substructures in
T^{\prime\prime}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
X_{C(b_{i})},X_{C(b_{i+1})}\in A
for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. The induced subgraph on
B\cup\{a\}
is the following branch:
b_{1}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
a
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
b_{n}

Let
j
be the smallest index such that
X_{C(a),C(b_{j})}\in A
. Reposition
a
along the branch such that
N(a)
is in between
N(b_{j-1})
and
N(b_{j})
where
a
becomes the root if
j=1
. Then, for all
i\geq j
, we have
X_{C(a),C(b_{i})}\in A
by Lemma
5.1
, so we obtain a desired modification of
T^{\prime\prime}
that now belongs to
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
.
∎

Let
S(X_{n})
denote the set of subsets
A\subseteq X_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
satisfies Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
,
5.3
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
denote the set of Fraïssé subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
excluding
\mathfrak{T}_{\leq 1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{\leq 2}
. The above construction establishes a map
\Phi:S(X_{n})\to\mathcal{F}_{n}
given by
\Phi(A)=\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
(where
\Phi(\varnothing)=\varnothing
).

Proposition 5.5
.

The map
\Phi
is a surjection.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
be a Fraïssé subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
such that its set of three-element structures is
A
. By the definition of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
, we have
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
. On the other hand, let
T
be a tree structure in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
. We show by induction on
|T|
that
T\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. This is clear for
|T|\leq 3
by taking induced substructures in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, so suppose
|T|\geq 4
. It is easy to see that there must exist leaves
a
and
b
such that
a
and
b
are separated, i.e.,
N(a)
and
N(b)
are neither the same nor adjacent. By Proposition
4.1
,
T
is the unique amalgamation of
T\setminus a
and
T\setminus b
, yet
|T\setminus a|=|T\setminus b|=|T|-1
, so by the inductive hypothesis,
T\setminus a,T\setminus b\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, implying that
T\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
by
d
. Thus,
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, so we have
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
. This means that given any Fraïssé class in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, its preimage under
\Phi
exists and is determined by its three-element structures.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

The map
\Phi
is injective.

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}_{A}
.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

The map
\Phi
is bijective.

As a result, each Fraïssé subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
is determined by its three-element structures, i.e., a subset of
X_{n}
satisfying Lemmas
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.3
. We introduce an additional result to enumerate these subclasses, which is the following.

Proposition 5.8
.

For each
n\geq 2
, let
f(n)
denote the number of Fraïssé subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
with
n
colors. Then, we have

(5.1)

f(n)=nf(n-1)+\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{m}f(m)

where
f(0)=1
,
f(1)=2
.

Proof.

Since the empty class is Fraïssé, we have
f(0)=1
. Let

\varepsilon(i,j):=\mathbf{1}_{X_{i,j}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}

for some Fraïssé subclass
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
. For
n\geq 1
, by Propositions
5.5
and
5.6
,
f(n)
is equal to the number of maps
\varepsilon:[n]\times[n]\to\{0,1\}
satisfying the following properties for all pairwise distinct
i,j,k\in[n]
:

(1)

If
\varepsilon(i,j)=\varepsilon(j,k)=1
then
\varepsilon(i,k)=1
.

(2)

If
\varepsilon(i,j)=\varepsilon(j,i)=1
, then
\varepsilon(i,i)=\varepsilon(j,j)=1
.

(3)

We have (
\varepsilon(i,j),\varepsilon(j,i))\neq(0,0)
.

Property (3) guarantees that all
n
colors appear in the counted subclass. We say that a nonempty subset
U
of
[n]
is
self-connected
if
\varepsilon(i,j)=\varepsilon(j,i)=1
for all
i,j\in U
and
isolated
if
\varepsilon(i,k)=0
for all
k\in[n]\setminus U
. We claim that there exists a self-connected and isolated set
U
for each
\varepsilon
. Note that
U
is unique if it exists, and Lemma
5.3
ensures that the case
i=j
is consistent with the properties of
U
.

To prove the claim, we proceed by induction on
n
. The result is clear for
n\leq 2
, so suppose
n\geq 3
. Fix a map
\varepsilon
, and consider any self-connected
W\subseteq[n]
. If
\varepsilon(i,k)=0
for some
i\in W
and all
k\in[n]\setminus W
, then
W
is our desired choice, so suppose there is
k
such that
\varepsilon(i,k)=1
. We also suppose
W
is
maximally self-connected
, meaning that
W
is not properly contained in any larger self-connected set. This implies that
\varepsilon(k,i)=0
, since otherwise Lemma
5.1
implies that
W\cup\{k\}
is self-connected. In fact, by Lemma
5.1
, we conclude that
(\varepsilon(w,k),\varepsilon(k,w))=(1,0)
for all
w\in W
. Let
K\subseteq[n]\setminus W
denote the set of such
k
. Observe that for all
l\in[n]\setminus(W\cup K)
, we have
(\varepsilon(w,l),\varepsilon(l,w))=(0,1)
, so it follows that
\varepsilon(l,k)=1
, while
\varepsilon(k,l)=0
by the maximal self-connectedness of
W
. Now, by the inductive hypothesis, there is a self-connected, isolated set
U\subseteq[n]\setminus W
. It is easy to see that
U\subseteq K
, meaning that
\varepsilon(u,w)=0
for all
u\in U
and
w\in W
, which gives the claim.

The right-hand side of Equation
5.1
is obtained by doing casework on
|U|=n-m
. Note that
|U|>0
since all
n
colors appear in the subclass. If
|U|=1
, suppose
U=\{u_{1}\}
. We have
\varepsilon(u_{1},u_{1})\in\{0,1\}
, while there are
f(n-1)
maps
\varepsilon:[n]\setminus\{u_{1}\}\times[n]\setminus\{u_{1}\}\to\{0,1\}
. There are
n
choices for
u_{1}
, contributing

2nf(n-1)=nf(n-1)+\binom{n}{n-1}f(n-1)

possible maps.

If
2\leq|U|\leq n
, then there are
f(n-|U|)=f(m)
maps
\varepsilon:[n]\setminus U\times[n]\setminus U\to\{0,1\}
while Lemma
5.3
implies that
\varepsilon(u,u)=1
for all
u\in U
. Since there are

\binom{n}{|U|}=\binom{n}{n-m}=\binom{n}{m}

choices for
U
, we have

\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}\binom{n}{m}f(m)

additional maps, and the proposition follows.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

The number of Fraïssé subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
for
n=1,2,3,4,\dots
with
n
colors is
2,9,61,551,\dots

Remark 5.10
.

The sequence
(f(n))_{n\geq 0}
appears in OEIS
[
15
, A006155]
. Its exponential generating function is

F(x)=\frac{1}{2-x-e^{x}}.

5.2.
Induced regular measures

Given a fixed measure
\mu
on a Fraïssé class
\mathfrak{F}
, let
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
be the subclass consisting of all structures
X
such that
\mu(X)\neq 0
(where
\mu(X)=\mu(\varnothing\to X)
) and
\mu^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\mu
to
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
. We say that
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
is the
support
of
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
is the
induced regular measure
on
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
. A subclass
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{F}
that is the support of some measure on
\mathfrak{F}
is called an
induced subclass
. The following proposition justifies the regularity of
\mu^{\prime}
; we include the proof for familiarity with induced regular measures.

Proposition 5.11

(
[
8
, Proposition 2.18, p.12]
)
.

If
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
is an induced subclass of
\mathfrak{F}
, then it is a Fraïssé class, and
\mu^{\prime}
is a regular measure on
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The argument follows that of
[
8
]
, which we include for completeness. Suppose that
X\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
and there is an embedding
i:Y\to X
(which could be an isomorphism). We have
\mu(X)=\mu(i)\mu(Y)
, so
\mu(X)\neq 0
implies that
\mu(Y)\neq 0
, or
Y\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
. Hence
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
satisfies
a
and
b
.

Clearly,
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
satisfies
c
since it is a subclass of
\mathfrak{F}
, so it suffices to show
d
. Given structures
X,Y\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
with a common substructure
Z
, suppose
Z^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Z^{\prime}_{m}
are all amalgamations of
X
and
Y
over
Z
. We have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mu(Z\to X)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu(Y\to Z^{\prime}_{i}).

Since the left hand side is nonzero, there must exist some
i
such that
\mu(Y\to Z^{\prime}_{i})
is nonzero, so
Z^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
, as desired.

Now we show that
\mu^{\prime}
defines a measure on
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
. Properties
a
and
b
are obvious. Observe that
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
simply excludes the structures
Z_{k}^{\prime}
where
\mu(Z_{k}^{\prime})=\mu(Y\to Z_{k}^{\prime})=0
, which are just the terms on the right-hand side of Equation
5.2
that vanish, so the equation still holds. Thus
c
is also satisfied.

Finally, given an embedding
i:Y\to X
where
X,Y\in\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
, we have
\mu^{\prime}(i)=\mu^{\prime}(X)\mu^{\prime}(Y)^{-1}
, which is nonzero, so
\mu^{\prime}
is indeed regular on
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
.
∎

From Theorem
4.15
, let
\mu
be a
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{2}\right]
-valued measure, i.e.,
\mu:\Theta(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n))\to\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{2}\right]
is a ring homomorphism realizing one of the
(2n+2)^{n}
measures on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
is an induced subclass with induced regular measure
\mu^{\prime}
. In particular, by Propositions
5.5
and
5.6
,
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is determined by its three-element structures. We narrow down the list of possible three-element structures of supports with the following result.

Lemma 5.12
.

For all distinct
i,j\in[n]
, at most one of
X_{i,j}
and
X_{j,i}
is in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
\mu^{\prime}
is regular on
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, if both
X_{i,j}
and
X_{j,i}
belong to
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, then
\mu^{\prime}(C(i,j))
and
\mu^{\prime}(C(j,i))
are both nonzero. As a result,
\mu^{\prime}(C(i,j)C(j,i))
is nonzero, contradicting
4.8c
.
∎

Let
B
be the two-leaf tree structure with node colored
k
. Recall that
A=[\varnothing\to\bullet]
and
B(k)=[\bullet\to B]
are isomorphism classes of embeddings in
\Theta(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n))
.

Lemma 5.13
.

For each
n\geq 1
, we have
\mu(A)\neq 0
for all measures
\mu:\Theta(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n))\to\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Again let
\mathbf{X}
denote
\mu(X)
. The proof idea is the same as that in Propositions
4.9
and
4.12
by showing that the coefficient matrix of the
\mathbf{S}(i)
is the adjacency matrix of a DAG with nonzero diagonal terms (so the DAG has self-loops adjoined). Indeed, suppose for the sake of contradiction that

\mathbf{A}=1+\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{B}(i)=0.

It follows that

\displaystyle 2\mathbf{S}(i)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}(i)+3\mathbf{C}(i,i)+1+\sum_{p\in[n]\setminus i}\mathbf{B}(p)-\sum_{p\in[n]\setminus i}\mathbf{C}(p,i)

\displaystyle=3\mathbf{C}(i,i)-\sum_{p\in[n]\setminus i}\mathbf{C}(p,i).

Let
\mathbf{M}=(m_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)\times(n-1)}
and again
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{S}(i))_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
where

\displaystyle m_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2-3\delta_{\mathbf{C}(i,i),\mathbf{S}(i)}&\text{if }i=j,\\[6.0pt]
\delta_{\mathbf{C}(j,i),\mathbf{S}(j)}&\text{if }i\neq j.\end{cases}

We thus obtain the matrix equation
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{0}
. Since
\mathbf{C}(i,j)\mathbf{C}(j,i)=0
for all distinct
i,j
,
G_{\mathbf{M}}
, the induced graph from
\mathbf{M}
, is directed. To see that
G_{\mathbf{M}}
has no cycles of length at least two, note that if there was a cycle
a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{l}\to a_{1}
for
l\geq 2
, then
(\mathbf{C}(a_{i},a_{i+1}),(\mathbf{C}(a_{i+1},a_{i}))=(0,\mathbf{S}(a_{i+1}))
for
i=1,\dots,l
where indices are taken mod
l
. Applying
4.8f
with
(i,j,k)=(a_{1},a_{i+1},a_{i+2})
for each
i
, we obtain
\mathbf{C}(a_{1},a_{i+1})=0
for all
i
, so
\mathbf{C}(a_{1},a_{l})=\mathbf{S}(a_{1})=0
, a contradiction. Hence
\mathbf{M}
is the adjacency matrix of a DAG with self-loops adjoined. By Lemma
4.10
, there is a permutation matrix
\mathbf{P}
such that
\mathbf{PMP^{-1}}
is upper triangular. Consequently,
\det(\mathbf{M})=\prod_{i\in[n]}m_{ii}=\pm 2^{k}\neq 0
for some nonnegative integer
k
, yet
\mathbf{s}\neq\mathbf{0}
by Proposition
4.9
, so the matrix equation admits no solutions and the conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

The above lemma ensures that induced subclasses are not automatically the empty Fraïssé class, since if
\mu(A)
vanishes, then
\mu(X)
vanishes for all
X
where
|X|\geq 1
.

Lemma 5.15
.

A color
k
appears in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mu(B(k))\neq 0
.

Proof.

Recall that
B
denotes the two-leaf tree structure with node colored
k
. Notice that
k
appears in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
if and only if
B\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, or
\mu(B)\neq 0
. Since
\mu(B)=\mu(\varnothing\to B)=\mu(A)\mu(B(k))
, and
\mu(A)
is nonzero by Lemma
5.13
,
\mu(B)
is nonzero if and only if
\mu(B(k))
is nonzero, which implies the desired result.
∎

Observe that if
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is a subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
, then it is also a subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(m)
for any
m\geq n
. If the colors
1,\dots,n
all appear in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, then
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
can be viewed as the minimal Fraïssé class of node-colored rooted binary trees that contains
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. The following proposition implies that given an induced (Fraïssé) subclass
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
for minimal
n
, the collection of all induced regular measures restricted from
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
accounts for all induced regular measures on
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. In other words, for each
n
, it suffices to consider induced subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
with
n
colors when computing induced regular measures.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
be a Fraïssé subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
with
n
colors and
\mu^{\prime}
be an induced regular measure on
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. If
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is the support of
\nu
where
\nu
is a measure on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(m)
for any
m\geq n
, then
\nu|_{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=\mu^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Recall that computing a measure is equivalent to solving the linear system encoded by
\mathbf{D}
in Equation
4.4
with appropriate constraints on the entries of
\mathbf{D}
. Since
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is the support of
\nu
, the colors
n+1,n+2,\dots,m
do not appear in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. It follows from Lemma
5.15
that for all
j\in[m]\setminus[n]
, we have
\nu(B(j))=0
. By
4.8e
, we obtain
\nu(B(i)C(j,i))=0
for all
i\in[n]
, yet
i
appears in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, so
\nu(B(i))\neq 0
, which means
\nu(C(j,i))=0
for all
i\in[n]
and
j\in[m]\setminus[n]
. It follows that
d_{ij}=0
for all such
i,j
, so the coefficient matrix
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
corresponding to
\nu
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}d_{11}&\dotsc&d_{1n}&0&\dotsc&0\\
d_{21}&\dotsc&d_{2n}&0&\dotsc&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
d_{n1}&\dotsc&d_{nn}&0&\dotsc&0\\
d_{(n+1)1}&\dotsc&d_{(n+1)n}&d_{(n+1)(n+1)}&\dotsc&d_{(n+1)m}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
d_{m1}&\dotsc&d_{mn}&d_{m(n+1)}&\dotsc&d_{mm}\end{pmatrix}.

Notice that the leading principal matrix of order
n
of
\mathbf{D}
encodes precisely the constraints on
\mu^{\prime}
, while the upper-right off diagonal block is a zero submatrix, so
\nu
is subject to the exact same constraints on the colors
1,\dots,n
as
\mu^{\prime}
(with potentially more constraints on colors
n+1,\dots,m
), implying that
\nu|_{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=\mu^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

By the above proposition, it suffices to consider Fraïssé subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
where all
n
colors appear. For each
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{n}
denote the set of Fraïssé subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
with
n
colors such that exactly one of
X_{i,j}
and
X_{j,i}
is in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
for all
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{n}
.

Proposition 5.17
.

A subclass
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
with
n
colors is an induced subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
if and only if
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{n}
.

Proof.

Since exactly one of
X_{i,j}
and
X_{j,i}
is in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, there is an induced total order on
[n]
where
i\prec j
if and only if
X_{i,j}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
X_{i,j}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
if and only if
i<j
. For all
i,j
, let

(\mathbf{B}(i),\mathbf{C}(i,j),\mathbf{C}(j,i))=(\mathbf{S}(i),\mathbf{S}(i),0)

and

\mathbf{C}(i,i)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{S}(i)&\text{if }X_{i,i}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The above construction satisfies
4.8
and defines the unique induced regular measure on
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
(since
\mathbf{S}(i)\neq 0
) as desired.

On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\not\in\mathcal{F}_{n}^{\prime}
, then by Lemma
5.2
, there exist
i,j
such that
X_{i,j}
and
X_{j,i}
are both in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, which by Lemma
5.12
means that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is not the support of any measure.
∎

Corollary 5.18
.

For each
n\geq 1
, there are
2^{n}n!
induced subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
with
n
colors.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.17
, it suffices to count the number of classes in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{n}
. There are
n!
total orderings of the
n
colors, and for each
i\in[n]
, we can either have
X_{i,i}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
or
X_{i,i}\not\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, giving
2^{n}
independent choices. Consequently, we obtain
2^{n}n!
distinct classes in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
2^{n}
classes up to color-swapping automorphisms, each admitting an induced regular measure.
∎

We now determine a formula for the induced regular measure on each of the induced (Fraïssé) subclasses with
n
colors. Without loss of generality, suppose that each subclass is
naturally ordered
; that is,
X_{i,j}
belongs to the subclass if and only if
i<j
. From now on, let each naturally ordered induced subclass with
n
colors be denoted as
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
for some
I\subseteq[n]
where
i\in I
if and only if
X_{i,i}\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
. Note that each
I
uniquely corresponds to a naturally ordered induced subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
with
n
colors by Corollary
5.7
. Let
\mu_{n}^{I}
denote the corresponding induced regular measure. For a tree
T
, let
\ell(T)
denote the number of leaves and
v_{T}(i)
denote the number of nodes of color
i
.

Proposition 5.19
.

For an induced subclass
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
where
I\subseteq[n]
and a tree
T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)_{I}^{\mathrm{ord}}
, we have

\mu_{n}^{I}(T)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\ell(T)=0,\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(P_{I}(i)^{v_{T}(i)}\,(1+P_{I}(i))^{\,1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{T}(j)}\Bigr)&\text{if }\ell(T)\geq 1,\end{cases}

where

P_{I}(i)=\frac{1}{1-3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I}(i)}.

Proof.

Fix a subset
I
, and, for convenience, let
\mu_{n}^{I}=\mu
and
P_{I}=P
. Recall the coefficient matrix
\mathbf{D}
from Proposition
4.12
, where
d_{ii}=2-3\delta_{\mathbf{C}(i,i),\mathbf{S}(i)}-\delta_{\mathbf{B}(i),\mathbf{S}(i)}
and
d_{ij}=\delta_{\mathbf{C}(j,i),\mathbf{S}(j)}-\delta_{\mathbf{B}(j),\mathbf{S}(j)}
for distinct
i,j
. By the unique construction given in the proof of Proposition
5.17
, we have

(\delta_{\mathbf{B}(i),\mathbf{S}(i)},\delta_{\mathbf{C}(i,j),\mathbf{S}(i)},\delta_{\mathbf{C}(j,i),\mathbf{S}(j)},\delta_{\mathbf{C}(i,i),\mathbf{S}(i)})=(1,1,0,\mathbf{1}_{I}(i))

for all
i,j
where
i<j
. This means that

\displaystyle d_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1-3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I}(i)&\text{if }i=j,\\[6.0pt]
-1&\text{if }i<j,\\[6.0pt]
0&\text{if }i>j.\end{cases}

In other words,
\mathbf{D}
is an upper triangular matrix with diagonal entries equal to
1-3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{I}(i)
and all entries above the diagonal equal to
-1
. The system
\mathbf{Ds}=\mathbf{1}
thus encodes the linear system

d_{ii}\mathbf{S}(i)-\sum_{j>i}\mathbf{S}(j)=d_{ii}\mu(S(i))-\sum_{j>i}\mu(S(j))=1

over all
i\in[n]
. By inducting downwards with base case
\mu(S(n))=\frac{1}{d_{nn}}
, we obtain

\mu(S(i))=\frac{1}{d_{ii}}\prod_{j=i+1}^{n}\frac{d_{jj}+1}{d_{jj}}=P(i)\prod_{j=i+1}^{n}\left(1+P(j)\right)

for all
i
. Now recall that

\mu(A)=\mu([\varnothing\to\bullet])=1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(B(i)).

Notice that by letting

Q(i)=\prod_{j=i}^{n}(1+P(j)),

we have

\mu(S(i))=Q(i)-Q(i+1),

which implies that

\displaystyle\mu(A)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(S(i))

\displaystyle=Q(1)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+P(j)).

From the value of
\mu
on irreducible marked structures, we now prove the general formula for
\mu
. We induct on
\ell(T)
.

If
\ell(T)=0
, then
\mu(T)=\mu(\varnothing\to\varnothing)=1
, and if
\ell(T)=1
, then indeed
\mu(T)=\mu(A)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+P(j))
. If
\ell(T)\geq 2
, then

\mu(T)=\mu(T\setminus a)\mu([T,a])

for some leaf
a\in T
where
(T,a)
denotes the embedding
T\setminus a\to T
. Let
c(a)\in[n]
denote the color of
N(a)
(the unique node adjacent to
a
). We split cases based on the position of
a
in
T
.

If
N(a)
is the root, then either
[T,a]=B(c(a))
or
[T,a]=C(c(a),j)
where
j
is the color of the node closest to the root. In both cases, we have

\displaystyle\mu(T)

\displaystyle=\mu(T\setminus a)\cdot\mu(S(c(a))

\displaystyle=\mu(T\setminus a)\cdot\left(P(C(a))\prod_{j=c(a)+1}^{n}(1+P(j))\right)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(P(C(a))^{v_{T}(c(a))-1}\prod_{i\in[n]\setminus c(a)}P(i)^{v_{T}(i)}\prod_{i=1}^{c(a)}(1+P(i))^{1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{T}(j)}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\prod_{i=c(a)+1}^{n}(1+P(i))^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{T}(j)}\Bigr)\Bigl(P(C(a))\prod_{j=c(a)+1}^{n}(1+P(j))\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(P(i)^{v_{T}(i)}(1+P(i))^{1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}v_{T}(j)}\Bigr),

which is the stated expression.

If
N(a)
is not the root and adjacent to only one other node, say, of color
j<c(a)
(as
X_{j,c(a)}\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I})
, then
[T,a]=D(j,c(a))=B(c(a))-C(c(a),j)
by Corollary
4.4
, so

\displaystyle\mu([T,a])

\displaystyle=\mu(B(c(a))-C(c(a),j))

\displaystyle=\mu(S(c(a))

\displaystyle=P(C(a))\prod_{j=c(a)+1}^{n}(1+P(j)),

and the rest of the computation is the same as the case above.

Finally, if
N(a)
is adjacent to two nodes, then we do casework on the colors of these two nodes. Here we illustrate the case where they both also have color
c(a)
; the other cases can be checked similarly using the relationships in Corollary
4.4
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle[T,a]

\displaystyle=E(c(a),c(a),c(a))

\displaystyle=-1-\sum_{p\in[n]}D(c(a),p)

\displaystyle=C(c(a),c(a))+D(c(a),c(a))-B(c(a),c(a))

\displaystyle=2C(c(a),c(a))-S(c(a)),

so again
\mu([T,a])=\mu(S(c(a))
as desired, completing the induction.
∎

Remark 5.20
.

If we assume another induced total ordering
\sigma\in S_{n}
(so the subclass is not necessarily naturally ordered), then we alternatively have

\mu_{n}^{I}(S(i))=P_{I}(i)\prod_{j=\sigma(i)+1}^{n}(1+P_{I}(j)).

Remark 5.21
.

We note an interesting occurrence of the lexicographic and wreath products. Suppose
I=[n]
. Let
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{\mathrm{ord}}
denote the Fraïssé limits of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)
and
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
, respectively. If
\Omega_{1}^{n\circ}
denotes the
n
-iterated lexicographic product of
\Omega_{1}
, then one can check that
\Omega_{\mathrm{ord}}\cong\Omega_{1}^{\circ n}
. Consequently, if
\mathrm{Aut}(\Omega_{1})^{\wr n}
denotes the
n
-iterated wreath product of
\mathrm{Aut}(\Omega_{1})
, then
\mathrm{Aut}(\Omega_{\mathrm{ord}})\cong\mathrm{Aut}(\Omega_{1})^{\wr n}
.

5.3.
The
n=2
case

We end the section by illustrating our classification results on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)
and assigning each of the
(2\cdot 2+2)^{2}=36
measures to their corresponding support.

Let
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
be an infinite Fraïssé subclass of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)
(if
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is finite, then it only contains trees with at most four leaves). By Corollary
5.7
, it suffices to determine the set of three-leaf trees in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
. Let
X_{1,1}
,
X_{1,2}
,
X_{2,1}
,
X_{2,2}
denote the following trees, from left to right
1
1
1
2
2
1
2
2

and recall that
\varepsilon(i,j)=\mathbf{1}_{X_{i,j}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}
. Notice that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is infinite if and only if
\varepsilon(i,i)\neq 0
for some
i
. Define the Fraïssé subclasses

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nt}\text{-}1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2):\text{every node not adjacent to a leaf has color }1\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nr}\text{-}1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2):\text{every non-root node has color }1\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nt}\text{-}2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2):\text{every node not adjacent to a leaf has color }2\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nr}\text{-}2}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2):\text{every non-root node has color }2\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{ord}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2):\text{no $2$ lies above a $1$ on any root-leaf path}\Bigr\},

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{rev}}

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2):\text{no $1$ lies above a $2$ on any root-leaf path}\Bigr\}.

Using Lemma
5.3
, we match the existence of three-leaf trees in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
and their corresponding Fraïssé subclasses:

\begin{array}[]{c c c c | c}\varepsilon(1,2)&\varepsilon(2,1)&\varepsilon(1,1)&\varepsilon(2,2)&\text{Subclass }\\
\hline\cr 0&0&1&0&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)\\
0&0&0&1&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)\\
1&0&1&0&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nt}\text{-}1}\\
1&0&0&1&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nr}\text{-}2}\\
1&0&1&1&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{ord}}\\
0&1&0&1&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nt}\text{-}2}\\
0&1&1&0&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{nr}\text{-}1}\\
0&1&1&1&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)^{\mathrm{rev}}\\
1&1&1&1&\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)\\
\end{array}

Given an induced regular measure
\mu^{\prime}
on
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, by Lemma
5.15
,
\varepsilon(i,j)=1
for distinct
i,j
if and only if
\mu^{\prime}(B(i)),\mu^{\prime}(B(j))
, and
\mu^{\prime}(C(i,j))
are all nonzero. Meanwhile,
\varepsilon(i,i)=1
if and only if
\mu^{\prime}(B(i))
and
\mu^{\prime}(C(i,i))
are both nonzero.

We use the computer algebra system Macaulay 2
[
7
]
to analyze the polynomial ring
\mathbb{Q}[B(1),C(1,1),C(1,2),B(2),C(2,1),C(2,2)]
modulo the ideal
I
generated by the quadratic equations from Proposition
4.8
for
n=2
. The command
dim(R/I)
returns
0
and
degree(R/I)
returns
36
, so the sytem has
36
complex solutions counted with multiplicity. By calling the
rationalPoints
function, we obtain
36
distinct solutions with coordinates that are powers of two up to sign, corresponding to
36

\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{2}\right]
-valued measures on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)
, which verifies our theoretical results from Section
4
. In Appendix
A
, we list the values of each of the measures and their supports according to the above table.

Remark 5.22
.

By restricting each of the measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{8}
to their support, we recover the induced regular measure
\mu_{1}^{I}
on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}=\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)
where
I=\{1\}
. This is the only regular measure on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
across all
n\geq 1
.

6.
Applications to tensor categories

In this section, we construct families of semisimple tensor categories using the induced subclasses of
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
and induced regular measures from Section
5
. We first recall some background on tensor categories and the Harman–Snowden construction. We then utilize oligomorphic groups to prove a semisimplicity criterion for these categories. Finally, we specialize the construction to the family of induced subclasses
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
, producing infinite families of semisimple tensor categories of superexponential growth.

6.1.
Background on tensor categories

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field, and
\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathbb{K}
-linear symmetric monoidal category (with a bilinear tensor product) with monoidal unit
\mathds{1}
. An object
X
is said to be
rigid
if there is a dual object
X^{*}
equipped with an evaluation morphism
\mathrm{ev}_{X}:X^{*}\otimes X\to\mathds{1}
and coevaluation morphism
\mathrm{coev}_{X}:\mathds{1}\to X\otimes X^{*}
satisfying some properties
[
4
, §2.10]
. A category is rigid if all of its objects are rigid, and
locally finite
if all objects have finite length and all Hom spaces are finite-dimensional.

Definition 6.1
.

A
tensor category
is a
\mathbb{K}
-linear symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
that is abelian, locally finite, rigid, and
\mathrm{End}(\mathds{1})=\mathbb{K}
.

An important property of tensor categories is their growth, which is defined based on their objects. For an object
X
, let
length
refer to its Jordan–Holder length.

Definition 6.2
.

A tensor category
\mathcal{C}
is of
subexponential growth
if for every object
X\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists a natural number
N_{X}
such that
\mathrm{length}(X^{\otimes n})\leq(N_{X})^{n}
for each
n\geq 0
. Otherwise,
\mathcal{C}
is of
superexponential growth
.

6.2.
The Harman–Snowden construction

Suppose we have a Fraïssé class
\mathfrak{F}
equipped with a
\mathbb{C}
-valued measure
\mu
. We now recall the Harman–Snowden construction
[
9
]
, which produces the
\mathbb{C}
-linear rigid symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{Perm}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
.

For each structure
X\in\mathfrak{F}
, let the formal symbol
\mathrm{Vec}_{X}
be the corresponding object. Given two objects
\mathrm{Vec}_{X}
and
\mathrm{Vec}_{Y}
, let

\mathrm{Hom}(X,Y)=\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{Amalg}(X,Y)]

where
\mathrm{Amalg}(X,Y)
denotes the set of isomorphism classes of amalgamations of
X
and
Y
. In other words,
\mathrm{Hom}(X,Y)
is the
\mathbb{C}
-module freely generated by isomorphism classes of amalgamations of
X
and
Y
, of which there are finitely many.

Now suppose
Y_{1,2}
is an amalgamation of
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathfrak{F}
and
Y_{2,3}
is an amalgamation of
X_{2},X_{3}\in\mathfrak{F}
. Let
\varphi_{1,2}
and
\varphi_{2,3}
denote the corresponding morphisms
\mathrm{Vec}_{X_{1}}\to\mathrm{Vec}_{X_{2}}
and
\mathrm{Vec}_{X_{2}}\to\mathrm{Vec}_{X_{3}}
. Composition is given by

\varphi_{2,3}\circ\varphi_{1,2}=\sum_{Y_{1,3}}\sum_{Z}\mu(Y_{1,3}\subset Z)\cdot\varphi_{1,3}

where
Y_{1,3}
ranges over all amalgamations of
X_{1}
and
X_{3}
and
Z
ranges over all amalgamations of
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
, and
X_{3}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1,2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2,3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1,3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{3}}}

Here, all the arrows denote embeddings in
\mathfrak{F}
. The above construction bilinearly extends to arbitrary compositions. We define
\mathbf{Perm}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
to be the additive category freely generated by the objects
\{\mathrm{Vec}_{X}\}
, where morphisms extend uniquely by additivity. There is a tensor product
\mathbf{\otimes}
on
\mathbf{Perm}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
where

\mathrm{Vec}_{X}\,\mathbf{\otimes}\,\mathrm{Vec}_{Y}=\bigoplus_{Z\in\mathrm{Amalg}(X,Y)}\mathrm{Vec}_{Z},

which has the expected tensor properties
[
9
]
.

The unit object is
\mathds{1}=\mathrm{Vec}_{\varnothing}
, and all objects are self-dual with evaluation (which acts on summands) and coevaluation given by projection onto and inclusion of the diagonal amalgamation, respectively. The action of
\otimes
on morphisms allows rigidity properties to hold; again, see
[
8
]
for further details. It follows from the construction that the categorical dimension of
\mathrm{Vec}_{X}
is
\mu(X)
, and the trace of an endomorphism equals
\mu(X)
for the diagonal amalgamation and vanishes otherwise.

We further recall one construction.

Definition 6.3
.

The
Karoubi envelope

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{kar}}
of a category
\mathcal{C}
has objects given by
(X,e)
, where
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
e:X\to X
is an idempotent. A morphism
(X,e)\to(Y,f)
is a morphism
m:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
such that
m=f\circ m\circ e
, and composition is inherited from
\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 6.4
.

For concreteness, we work over
\mathbb{C}
in this paper. All constructions and statements (including those for measures) remain valid over any field
k
of characteristic
\neq 2
with identical proofs.

6.3.
The oligomorphic group perspective

For a Fraïssé class
\mathfrak{F}
, let
\Omega
be its Fraïssé limit. From our discussion in Section
2
, the group
G=\mathrm{Aut}(\Omega)
with its natural action on
\Omega
is oligomorphic. There is a relative version of
G
, which is
(G,\mathscr{E})
, the class of transitive
\mathscr{E}
-smooth
G
-sets where
\mathscr{E}
is the collection of pointwise stabilizers of finite subsets of
\Omega
. Returning to this perspective allows us to establish category-theoretical results applicable to Fraïssé classes and measures on them.

Recall that given a measure
\mu
on
\mathfrak{F}
, there is a unique corresponding measure
\mu^{*}
on
(G,\mathscr{E})
such that if
i:X\to Y
is an embedding in
\mathfrak{F}
inducing a map of finitary
\mathscr{E}
-smooth
G
sets
i^{*}:\Omega^{Y}\to\Omega^{X}
, where
\Omega^{X}
is the set of embeddings
X\to\Omega
, then
\mu^{*}(i^{*})=\mu(i)
. We similarly define the
\mathbb{C}
-linear rigid symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E};\mu^{*})
. Its objects are
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathscr{X}}
where
\mathscr{X}
is a finitary
\mathscr{E}
-smooth
G
-set; morphisms
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathscr{X}}\to\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathscr{Y}}
are given by
G
-invariant
\mathscr{Y}\times\mathscr{X}
matrices where composition is given by matrix multiplication relying on
\mu^{*}
that follows a theory of integration developed in
[
9
, §7]
. The category’s tensor product is defined on objects by
\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathscr{X}}\otimes\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathscr{Y}}=\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathscr{X}\times\mathscr{Y}}
. By
[
9
, Theorem 6.9, p.45]
, there is an equivalence of categories

\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E};\mu^{*})\cong\mathbf{Perm}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)

where
\mathrm{Vec}_{X}
corresponds to
\mathrm{Vec}_{\Omega^{X}}
, and
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{Vec}_{X},\mathrm{Vec}_{Y})
corresponds to

\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{Vec}_{\Omega^{X}},\mathrm{Vec}_{\Omega^{Y}})
via identifying
G
-orbits on
\Omega^{Y}\times\Omega^{X}
with amalgamations of
X
and
Y
. Consequently, we can translate properties of
(G,\mathscr{E})
and
\mathfrak{F}
back and forth, which is especially useful for certain results that are more conveniently considered from the former’s perspective.

6.4.
Semi-simplicity criterion

We now introduce a criterion for semisimple tensor categories that arise from Fraïssé classes.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\mu
be regular measure on a Fraïssé class
\mathfrak{F}
valued in
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
for some integer
N\geq 1
. If every prime divisor of
|\mathrm{Aut}(X)|
divides
N
for every structure
X\in\mathfrak{F}
, then the Karoubi envelope of
\mathbf{Perm}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
, denoted
\mathbf{Rep}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
, is a semisimple tensor category.

Let
\Omega
be the Fraïssé limit of
\mathfrak{F}
, and
G
be the automorphism group of
\Omega
. We work with the corresponding measure
\mu^{*}
on
(G,\mathscr{E})
, which is also
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
-valued. Let
\mathscr{E}^{+}
be the set of all open subgroups of
G
that contain some member of
\mathscr{E}
with finite index.

Lemma 6.6
.

Every
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
-valued regular measure
\mu^{*}
on
(G,\mathscr{E})
extends uniquely to a
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
-valued regular measure
\mu^{+}
on
(G,\mathscr{E}^{+})
.

Proof.

We follow the proofs of
[
13
, Theorem 2.15]
and
[
8
, Lemma 4.4]
. Let
V\in\mathscr{E^{+}}
. By definition, there is some finite
A\subset\Omega
such that if
X
is the algebraic closure of
A
, then we have the finite-index containments

G(X)\subset G(A)\subset V\subset G[X]

where
G[X]
is the subgroup of
G
fixing
X
as a set. It follows that

\mu^{+}(G/V)=[V:G(X)]^{-1}\cdot\mu^{*}(G/G(X)).

Since every prime divisor of
|\mathrm{Aut}(X)|=[G[X]:G(X)]
divides
N
, so does every prime divisor of
[V:G(X)]
, implying that
\mu^{+}(G/V)
is a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
-valued measure. But
\mu^{*}
is regular, so
\mu^{+}(G/V)
is a unit in
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
and
\mu^{+}
is also regular, as desired.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Note that the previous Lemma shows an isomorphism

\Theta(G,\mathscr{E})\left[1/N\right]\cong\Theta(G,\mathscr{E}^{+})\left[1/N\right],

and therefore, if one considers more general ring-valued measures, we have the equality of corresponding measures as long as
N
is invertible in the ring.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

From Lemma
6.6
, consider the rigid symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E}^{+};\mu^{+})
where
\mu^{+}
is a regular
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
-valued measure on
(G,\mathscr{E}^{+})
that restricts to a regular measure
\mu^{*}
on
(G,\mathscr{E})
. Since
\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{N}\right]
can be reduced mod
p
for all
p\nmid N
, and
\mathscr{E}^{+}
is upward closed under finite-index containment, we apply
[
9
, Theorem 7.11]
to obtain that nilpotent endomorphisms in the category have categorical trace
0
. Combined with the regularity of
\mu^{+}
, by
[
9
, Proposition 7.19]
, endomorphism algebras in
\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E}^{+};\mu^{+})
are semisimple. It follows that endomorphism algebras in
\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E};\mu^{*})
, which is a full subcategory of
\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E}^{+};\mu^{+})
, are also semisimple. This implies that the Karoubi envelope of
\mathbf{Perm}(G,\mathscr{E};\mu^{*})
is a semisimple tensor category. The proposition thus follows from our discussion in
6.3
.
∎

Finally, given a (not necessarily regular) measure
\mu
with support
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
and induced regular measure
\mu^{\prime}
, we can relate their two resulting categories through a process called semi-simplification
[
5
, §2.3]
as follows.

Proposition 6.8

(
[
8
, Corollary 4.6, p.24]
)
.

If
\mathbf{Rep}(\mathfrak{F}^{\prime};\mu^{\prime})
is semisimple, then nilpotent endormophisms in
\mathbf{Rep}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
have categorical trace
0
, and
\mathbf{Rep}(\mathfrak{F}^{\prime};\mu^{\prime})
is the semi-simplification of
\mathbf{Rep}(\mathfrak{F};\mu)
.

6.5.
Results

Recall the collection of induced subclasses
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
with
n
colors each consisting of trees where colors monotonically increase along every root-leaf path and containing
X_{i,i}
if and only if
i\in I
. There are
2^{n}
of these induced subclasses, each equipped with an induced regular measure
\mu_{n}^{I}
. Below are the central constructions of our paper, which we have worked towards in the past sections.

Theorem 6.9
.

For each integer
n\geq 1
and each subset
I\subseteq[n]
, the category
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I};\mu^{I}_{n})
is a semisimple tensor category.

We will show the above theorem by applying Proposition
6.5
. Since
\mu_{n}^{I}
is always regular on its induced subclass, it suffices to check the semisimplicity criterion.

Lemma 6.10
.

For each
n\geq 1
and
T\in\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)
, we have
|\mathrm{Aut}(T)|=2^{k}
for some nonnegative integer
k
.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{Aut}(X)
denote the automorphism group of the entire tree (which also includes nodes) preserving edge relations and relation
S
. Note that
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(X)
, so it suffices to show that
|\mathrm{Aut}(X)|
is a power of two.

We proceed by induction on
|X|
. For the base case
|X|=1
,
X
is the one-leaf tree where the only automorphism is the identity map, so
|\mathrm{Aut}(X)|=1
. Now, given a tree
T
, suppose its root branches out to subtrees
U
and
V
:
U
V

If
U\not\cong V
, then
\mathrm{Aut}(X)
acts on
U
and
V
separately, so
\mathrm{Aut}(X)\cong\mathrm{Aut}(U)\times\mathrm{Aut}(V)
. Since
|U|,|V|<|X|
, by the inductive hypothesis,

|\mathrm{Aut}(X)|=|\mathrm{Aut}(U)||\mathrm{Aut}(V)|

is indeed a power of two.

If
U\cong V
, then there is an automorphism
s
swapping the children of the root, thus also swapping
U
and
V
. Notice that
\{1,s\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
permutes the normal subgroup
\mathrm{Aut}(U)\times\mathrm{Aut}(V)\unlhd\mathrm{Aut}(X)
, so we have

\mathrm{Aut}(T)\cong(\mathrm{Aut}(U)\times\mathrm{Aut}(V))\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

which implies that

|\mathrm{Aut}(X)|=2|\mathrm{Aut}(U)||\mathrm{Aut}(V)|.

This is again a power of two by the inductive hypothesis.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
6.9
. For each subset
I
of
[n]
, let
\tilde{\mu}^{I}_{n}
denote an extension of
\mu^{I}_{n}
to
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)\supseteq\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
.

Corollary 6.11
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer. Then nilpotent endomorphisms in
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n);\tilde{\mu}^{I}_{n})
have categorical trace
0
, and the semi-simplification of

\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n);\tilde{\mu}^{I}_{n})
is
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I};\mu^{I}_{n})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.9
,
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I};\mu^{I}_{n})
is a semisimple tensor category. The support of
\tilde{\mu}^{I}_{n}
is
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I}
, whose induced regular measure is
\mu^{I}_{n}
, so the corollary follows from Proposition
6.8
.
∎

Finally, we explicitly show that our categories have superexponential growth, which is an essential property of tensor categories arising from measures.

Proposition 6.12
.

For each
n\geq 1
and each nonempty
I\subseteq[n]
, the tensor category
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I};\mu^{I}_{n})
is of superexponential growth.

Proof.

We resolve the
n=1
case, from which the result easily follows for larger
n
. For each structure
X
, let
f_{m}(X)
be the number of ways to label the elements of
X
with
\{1,\dots,m\}
up to an automorphism (where every element has at least one label). For example, if
X
is the tree structure with two leaves, then
f_{m}(X)=2^{m-1}-1
for all
m\geq 2
. Let
T
be the one-leaf tree structure. By the definition of the tensor product, we find that

\mathrm{Vec}_{T}^{\otimes m}=\bigoplus_{|X|\leq m}\mathrm{Vec}_{X}^{\oplus f_{m}(X)}

where each copy of
\mathrm{Vec}_{X}
comes from a distinct choice of embeddings
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m}):T^{m}\to X
, which there are precisely
f_{m}(X)
of. Now, since
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)^{\mathrm{ord}}_{I};\mu^{I}_{1})
is a semisimple tensor category (Theorem
6.9
), the inequalities

\mathrm{length}(X^{\otimes m})\leq\dim\End(X^{\otimes m})\leq\mathrm{length}(X^{\otimes m})^{2}

hold for all objects
X
in the category, so we alternatively consider

\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Vec}_{T}^{\otimes m})=\mathrm{End}\left(\bigoplus_{|X|\leq m}\mathrm{Vec}_{X}^{\oplus f_{m}(X)}\right)=\bigoplus_{|X|,|X^{\prime}|\leq m}\mathrm{Hom}\left(\mathrm{Vec}_{X}^{\oplus f_{m}(X)},\mathrm{Vec}_{X^{\prime}}^{\oplus f_{m}(X^{\prime})}\right).

In particular,

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Vec}_{T}^{\otimes m})

\displaystyle\geq\dim\bigoplus_{|X|=|X^{\prime}|=m}\mathrm{Hom}\left(\mathrm{Vec}_{X}^{\oplus f_{m}(X)},\mathrm{Vec}_{X^{\prime}}^{\oplus f_{m}(X^{\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|X|=|X^{\prime}|=m}f_{m}(X)f_{m}(X^{\prime})\left|\mathrm{Amalg}(X,X^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left(\sum_{|X|=m}f_{m}(X)\right)^{2}.

Observe that the sum inside parentheses is just the number of labeled
m
-element structures in
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(1)
, which is equal to
(2m-3)!!=(2m-3)(2m-5)\cdots 3\cdot 1
by
[
15
, A001147]
. As a result, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{length}(X^{\otimes m})

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\dim\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Vec}_{T}^{\otimes m})}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{|X|=m}f_{m}(X)

\displaystyle=(2m-3)!!

\displaystyle=\exp{(\Theta(m\log m))},

which is superexponential growth.
∎

Remark 6.13
.

If
I
is empty, then
X_{i,i}\notin\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)_{I}^{\mathrm{ord}}
for all
i\in[n]
. As a result, its Fraïssé limit is finite (with
2^{n}
elements), so
\mathbf{Rep}(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(n)_{\varnothing}^{\mathrm{ord}};\mu_{n}^{\varnothing})
is a symmetric fusion category with exponential growth, and thus equivalent to the category of representations of an affine supergroup scheme by Deligne’s theorem.

Appendix A
Measures on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)
and their supports

In the table below, we list the values of each of the
36
measures on
\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2)
. In particular, the listed values are for the irreducible marked structures in
\Theta(\partial\mathfrak{T}_{3}(2))
that generate the rest of the measure ring. The last column indicates the supports of the measures (
5.2
,
5.3
), which is labeled as “Trivial” if the support is finite.

B(1)

C(1,1)

C(1,2)

B(2)

C(2,1)

C(2,2)

Support

\mu_{1}

-0.5
